## 1. Background information on the procedure

## 1.1. Type II variation

Pursuant to Article 16 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 1234/2008, Alfasigma S.p.A. submitted to the European Medicines Agency on 28 January 2026 an application for a variation.

The following changes were proposed:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                             | Type              |
|--------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| C.6.a                    | C.6.a) Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |

Extension of indication to include treatment of axial spondyloarthritis in adult patients with active radiographic axial spondyloarthritis (r-axSpA) and with active non-radiographic axial spondyloarthritis (nr-axSpA) for Jyseleca, based on interim results from study GLPG0634-CL-336 (OLINGUITO); this is a Phase 3 randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, parallel-group program to evaluate efficacy and safety of filgotinib in adult subjects with active axial spondyloarthritis. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.8, 5.1 and 5.2 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 7.1 of the RMP has also been submitted.

The variation requested amendments to the Summary of Product Characteristics, Annex II, Package Leaflet and to the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

## Information on paediatric requirements

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included (an) EMA Decision(s) EMA/PE/0000232302 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP EMA/PE/0000232302 was not yet completed as some measures were deferred.

The PDCO issued on 29 May 2019 a positive partial compliance check for PIP EMA/PE/0000232302, in relation to:

| Agreed studies/measures   | Description                                                                 |
|---------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| (study identifier)        |                                                                             |
| Study 3                   | Toxicity study of filgotinib and its metabolite GS-829845 in juvenile rats. |

## Information relating to orphan market exclusivity

## Similarity

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No. 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the MAH did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

## 2. Scientific discussion

## 2.1. Introduction

## 2.1.1. Problem statement

## Disease or condition

AxSpA is a chronic inflammatory disease primarily affecting the sacroiliac joints (SIJ) and spine. Historically, the term \"ankylosing spondylitis\" (AS) has been used to describe this specific inflammatory disease. However, the term is now considered outdated as it does not encompass the full spectrum of related conditions. AxSpA, as the preferred term, includes both radiographic (capturing the aforementioned AS patient group) and non-radiographic forms, reflecting a broader understanding of the disease.

AxSpA typically manifests in individuals under the age of 45 years, with a peak onset in the third decade of life. Historically, it was considered a male-dominated condition, but recent studies indicate a more balanced gender distribution, especially in nr-axSpA. The prevalence of axSpA varies globally, influenced by the distribution of the human leukocyte antigen B27 (HLA-B27) gene, which is strongly associated with the disease. In Europe, the prevalence ranges from approximately 0.32% to 0.70%, with higher percentages observed in populations with a higher prevalence of HLA-B27, such as Scandinavian countries (Siebert et al. 2016). The disease is characterised by inflammation at the entheses, leading to pain and stiffness. Over time, this inflammation can cause new bone formation and fusion of the affected joint(s), a condition known as ankylosis. The interleukin (IL)-23/IL-17 pathway has been identified as a critical component in the inflammatory process, with cytokines such as tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-alpha playing a significant role. When structural damage to joints is not necessarily visible with conventional radiology, a diagnosis is often based on clinical features, MRI findings, and the presence of the HLA-B27 gene; thus, defining so-called nr-axSpA. If the structural damage in the SIJ or spine is radiographically visible, the disease is termed r-axSpA. AxSpA is associated with significant pain and disability. In the early stages of the disease, active inflammation is the primary driver of symptoms, including pain, stiffness, fatigue, and poor quality of sleep. Over time, chronic inflammation can lead to structural changes, such as new bone formation, which contribute to loss of spinal mobility and impaired physical function. Despite lack of structural damage on the SIJ and spine, patients with nr-axSpA have comparable disease burden (disease activity, pain, impairments of physical function and quality of life) to those with AS. In both r-axSpA and nr-axSpA, many patients experience not only the core axial symptoms but also peripheral manifestations, including peripheral arthritis, enthesitis, and, to a lesser extent, dactylitis. In addition, extra-musculoskeletal manifestations, such as acute anterior uveitis, inflammatory bowel disease, and psoriasis are frequently observed and contribute to the overall disease burden and impaired quality of life. The presence of these diverse clinical features underscores the need for a comprehensive, patient-centered treatment approach that addresses both axial and peripheral disease domains as well as associated comorbidities. The treatment goals of axSpA are to reduce symptom severity, maintain spinal flexibility and normal posture, reduce functional limitations, maintain work ability, decrease disease complications, and to slow progression of structural damage. Current treatment options for axSpA aim to reduce inflammation, manage pain, and maintain mobility. NSAIDs are typically the first line of treatment for relief of pain and stiffness and are recommended by the joint Assessment of SpondyloArthritis international Society (ASAS)-European Alliance of Associations for Rheumatology (EULAR) 2022 guidelines, to provide relief from pain and stiffness. For patients who do not respond adequately to NSAIDs, biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs (bDMARDs) targeting TNF-alpha or IL-17 are commonly used e.g. adalimumab, bimekizumab, etanercept, and secukinumab. Other advanced therapies, including Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors (upadacitinib for nr-axSpA and r-axSpA, and tofacitinib for r-axSpA), are also an option for this patient group. Physical therapy and regular exercise are also crucial components of management, helping to preserve range of motion and improve overall function. In severe cases, surgical interventions may be necessary to correct bone deformities or/and relieve pain. Despite available treatments, a substantial proportion experience suboptimal responses to advanced therapies such as TNF inhibitors and IL-17 inhibitors which can ultimately lead to irreversible structural damage, further loss of spinal mobility, functional limitations, and substantial impact on daily living and overall quality of life (Navarro-Compán et al. 2025). This often necessitates treatment discontinuation or switching. However, such treatment changes generally yield diminishing clinical benefit over time. Further limitations of bDMARDs include the risk of immunogenicity, injection-site reactions, burdensome administration requirements, and delayed onset of action.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

These challenges underscore the need for a broader range of therapeutic options that can address the heterogeneity of axSpA and support individualised treatment strategies.

## Initially claimed therapeutic indication:

## Axial spondyloarthritis

## Non-radiographic axial spondyloarthritis (nr-axSpA)

Jyseleca is indicated for the treatment of active non-radiographic axial spondyloarthritis in adult patients with objective signs of inflammation as indicated by elevated C-reactive protein (CRP) and/or magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), who have responded inadequately or are intolerant to nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs).

## Ankylosing spondylitis (AS, radiographic axial spondyloarthritis [r-axSpA])

Jyseleca is indicated for the treatment of active ankylosing spondylitis (radiographic axial spondyloarthritis) in adult patients who have responded inadequately or are intolerant to conventional therapy.

## 2.1.2. About the product

Filgotinib is a JAK1 inhibitor. Pharmacological inhibition of JAK1 reduces pro-inflammatory cytokine signalling and has proven to be effective in a range of inflammatory conditions. Jyseleca (filgotinib), available as a 100 mg or 200 mg film-coated tablet for oral use, is currently approved in Europe for the treatment of rheumatoid arthritis (RA) and for ulcerative colitis (UC).

This application supports the extension of the filgotinib indication to adult patients with active AS (also known as r-axSpA) and nr-axSpA; both covered under the umbrella term axSpA.

## 2.1.3. The development programme/compliance with CHMP guidance/scientific advice

The Applicant received Scientific Advice on the development of Filgotinib maleate for the treatment of active radiographic axial spondyloarthritis (r-axSpA) and non-radiographic axial spondyloarthritis (nr axSpA) in adult patients who have responded inadequately to, or who are intolerant to conventional therapy from the CHMP on 23/06/2022 (EMA/SA/0000086316).

The Scientific Advice pertained to the following Clinical aspects:

- Design of Phase 3 axial SpA study (patient populations with 2 independent studies under a single protocol (radiographic and non-radiographic axial spondyloarthritis) and more specifically the inclusion/exclusion criteria, dosing approach, proposed control group, primary and key secondary endpoints and timing, hypothesis testing strategy and sample size
- Adequacy of overall planned data package and regulatory submission strategy

## 2.2. Non-clinical aspects

No new non-clinical data have been submitted in this application, which was considered acceptable by the CHMP.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.2.1. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

The environmental risk assessment (ERA) was previously submitted for filgotinib as part of the EU initial marketing authorisation application (MAA) for the treatment of rheumatoid arthritis (RA; procedure EMEA/H/C/005113/0000). The ERA was updated to support the extension of indication to add the treatment of ulcerative colitis (UC; procedure EMEA/H/C/005113/II/0001).

In the current procedure, the MAH has provided an ERA to include the new indication of axSpA. No new studies for the ERA were included with this application.

## Phase I

The Log Pow was 1.36 (pH 7) using the shake flask method (OECD 107). Although below the trigger value, it was decided to perform definitive PBT assessment. The conclusion of this was that filgotinib is very persistent in sediment (DT50 &gt; 180 days) and can be classified as very persistent (vP).

The PECSW value using a default Fpen was 1.28 µg/L which exceeded the action limit of 0.01 µg/L.

However, the MAH chose to use a refined PECSW value where the refined Fpen was based on worstcase prevalence for RA, UC and AxSpA (2.068 %) and a maximum daily dose of filgotinib of 225 mg. The refined PECSW value was calculated to be 2.64 µg/L and was used to re-calculate the Phase II Tier A and Tier B PEC/PNEC ratios.

## Phase II

For this application, the same PNEC values were presented as for the original ERA submitted for the MAA. In the table below the updated PEC/PNEC ratios are presented, based on the refined PEC value obtained for all three indications. The ratios for surfacewater, groundwater and microorganism compartments remain below 1, and the conclusion for these compartments remains: the clinical use of filgotinib is not expected to be a risk for the environment.

The PEC values in relevant environmental compartments are compared to the PNEC values for these compartments by calculation of PEC/PNEC ratios. The PEC value in sediment (dry) was recalculated with the updated PECSURFACEWATER and compared to the PNEC values for this compartment. As the ratio for sediment compartment is above 1 it indicates that there is a risk to sediment dwelling organisms.

## 2.2.2. Discussion on non-clinical aspects

Filgotinib was found to be very persistent in the sediment compartment but is not considered as a PBT or vPvB substance. Based on a complete Phase II assessment, it is concluded that no environmental effects are expected following the use of filgotinib, except for a risk to sediment dwelling organisms.

## 2.2.3. Conclusion on the non-clinical aspects

The non-clinical aspects of filgotinib were reviewed during the original approval procedure for filgotinib. No new non-clinical studies were submitted in support of the present application. This was acceptable.

Based on the updated data submitted in this application, the use of filgotinib in the new indication of axSpA poses an environmental risk to sediment dwelling organisms while for surfacewater, groundwater and microorganism compartments it did not lead to a significant risk for the environmental exposure. Therefore, considering the risk for sediment compartment (sediment dwelling organisms) and the potential risk for the environment, SmPC Sections 5.3 and 6.6 have been updated.

## 2.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## Methods

## Bioanalytical methods

Previously developed and validated bioanalytical methods were used for filgotinib and its major metabolite.

## Pharmacokinetics data analysis

Standard non-compartmental analysis was used to compile summary statistics of the PK of filgotinib and its major metabolite (GS-829845). In addition, model-based population PK analysis was used to further characterise the PK of filgotinib and GS-829845.

Pharmacokinetic Results - Study A and Study B

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Phase 3 study (OLINGUITO) included intensive PK substudies in both Study A (r-axSpA) and Study B (nr-axSpA). Serial blood samples were collected at predose and up to 6 h postdose for subjects in these PK substudies. The predose value was imputed to 24 h and data were analysed by standard noncompartmental analysis (NCA) to estimate PK parameters for filgotinib and GS-829845 in substudy participants.

A comparison of filgotinib and GS-829845 exposures between subjects with axSpA, RA, and UC in the Phase 3 PK substudies is provided in Table 4.

## Evaluation and qualification of models

A previously developed population PK model (Study number: GLPG0634-MS-019) was updated with data from Phase 3 study GLPG0634-CL-336 (axSpA patients, N=358) and the model was further refined.

The objective of the analysis was to characterise the population PK of filgotinib and GS-829845 in plasma following administration of filgotinib in axSpA patients, determine which covariates have significant impact on the PK of filgotinib and GS-829845 and to quantify their effects, and to explore exposure-response relationships between derived PK parameters and efficacy/safety endpoints.

The Stochastic Approximation Expectation-Maximization (SAEM) algorithm was used for parameter estimation. Standard error (SE) and OFV were obtained using the important sampling method.

## Stepwise model refinement

A structural population PK model (Figure 1, Study number: GLPG0634-MS-019) was developed based on data from six Phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers, four Phase 2 studies in Crohn's Disease (CD), Small Bowel Crohn's Disease, Fistulizing Crohn's Disease and axSpA (GLPG0634-CL-223) patients, respectively, and six Phase 3 studies in Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA), UC, CD. The objective was to characterise PK of filgotinib and GS-829845, and to identify covariates of interest.

The pharmacokinetics of filgotinib and GS-829845 were described using a joint parent- metabolite model with first-pass effect with dose apportionment, zero-order absorption processes with lag-time, unidirectional transformation from parent to metabolite, 2-compartments for distribution of filgotinib and 1-compartment for GS-829845, and a linear elimination for both analytes from the joint central compartment. A proportional error model and a combined error model were used for filgotinib and GS-

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

829845, respectively. This model described both the pre-systemic formation of metabolite and its subsequent absorption as well as the systemic formation and elimination of the metabolite. Baseline body weight, baseline eGFR, baseline CRP, sex, patient status, Asian race, food intake and concomitant administration of an ARA or a P-gp inducer were retained as covariates in the full model and their impact on PK exposure was derived from population estimates uncertainty. Body weight and eGFR were the most influential covariates.

<!-- image -->

## Figure 1. Structural parent-metabolite model

Building on these results, the full model was retrieved and subsequently simplified by removing noninformative covariates and retaining only those that were identified as clinically important based on pre-specified criteria (if the 95% CI of the Cmax and AUCτ ratios fell entirely outside the interval of 0.75-1.25). The only covariates found to meet the criteria for clinical importance were body weight (allometric scaling with fixed exponents of 0.75 and 1 on clearance and distribution parameters), eGFR, and dose on the fraction of the dose undergoing first-pass effect (Fd). Before including new study data from GLPG0634-CL-336 (Table 3) in the model building dataset, and the refined model could adequately predict GLPG0634-CL-336 concentrations. The preliminary model was able to predict the concentrations collected from axSpA patients, even if some misspecifications were observed. These misspecifications pointed toward adjustments in the covariate model or

updating the parameter estimates, a preliminary external evaluation was conducted to check whether variability components for the axSpA patient population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

After inclusion of Phase 3 study in axSpA patients (GLPG0634-CL-336), the dataset included 4775 subjects (4417 participants already included in the previous modelling exercise), including 358 patients from GLPG0634-CL-336. Out of the 33458 quantifiable concentration records (for which time of observation, time of dose, and dose amount are available) of filgotinib, 8.7% of the concentrations were below quantification limit (BQL). The newly added Phase 3 data comprised 775 quantifiable filgotinib concentrations of which 6.6% were BQL. Out of the 16753 quantifiable GS-829845 concentrations, 0.2% were BQL. The newly added Phase 3 data comprised 806 quantifiable GS-829845 concentrations of which 2.5% were BQL.

To reduce the long runtimes, a reduced dataset was used during the covariate analysis, including only data from subjects with rich PK sampling and data from the newly added Phase 3 axSpA patients. The additional covariates to be tested were limited to any relevant covariates for axSpA (age, race, sex, CRP at baseline, disease status) identified based on diagnostic plots and forest plots that were created using a univariate (considering only the uncertainty (SE) of fixed parameters) and multivariate approach (considering the uncertainty for fixed parameters, IIV and covariate distributions (accounting for their correlations)). The final model was re-run with the complete dataset to obtain final population PK parameters. The covariate exploration followed the full covariate modelling approach, and the selection of covariates to keep in the final model was based on the statistical Wald test (keep covariates with p-value &lt; 0.05).

The covariate re-assessment did not reveal any additional significant covariates. The univariate analysis highlighted an apparent effect of health status (1.40 [1.32-1.49] for axSpA; not stratified on nr-axSpA and r-axSpA) on filgotinib exposure parameter AUC0-Tss with confidence intervals extending beyond the standard bioequivalence range (0.8-1.25). In the multivariate analysis, which incorporates the IIV of the model, the point estimates were all close to unity, indicating no meaningful differences between the PK of filgotinib in subjects with axSpA as compared to subjects with RA, UC, or CD or HV.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The parameter estimates of the final model, when fitted to the full dataset (4766 participants), are presented in Table 4. All parameters of the final model were estimated with RSE% &lt;10%. The filgotinib and GS-829845 goodness-of-fit plots are presented in Figure 2. The pcVPC stratified on health status (healthy volunteers vs patients) are presented in Figure 3 and Figure 4, respectively. Figure 5 shows the pcVPC of only patients with axSpA patients for filgotinib and the metabolite. Other metrics, such as the condition number, parameter correlations, and the stability of the estimation, were also evaluated and found to be within acceptable ranges.

## Target population

Serial blood samples were collected at steady state at pre-dose and up to 6h post-dose for subjects in PK substudies as part of the Phase 2 (GLPG0634-CL-223; TORTUGA) study in AS. The 24h value was imputed using the pre-dose value, and data were analysed by standard NCA to estimate PK exposure metrics for filgotinib and GS-829845 in substudy participants.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Individual PK exposure metrics of both compounds at steady-state (AUC0-τ,ss, AUCeff and Cmax,ss), derived for axSpA patients from individual predictions from the final population PK model and obtained by NCA, are summarised in Table 7.

## 2.3.3. Pharmacodynamics

## Mechanism of action

According to the Jyseleca SmPC, filgotinib is a reversable inhibitor of the JAK family. JAKs are intracellular enzymes which transmit signals arising from cytokine or growth factor-receptor interactions on the cellular membrane. JAK1 is important in mediating inflammatory cytokine signals, JAK2 in mediating myelopoiesis and erythropoiesis and JAK3 plays critical roles in immune homeostasis and lymphopoiesis. Filgotinib modulates these signalling pathways and is claimed to preferentially inhibit the activity of JAK1.

No new data on the mechanism of action of filgotinib specifically in axSpA were submitted with this application.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Primary and secondary pharmacology

No additional primary or secondary pharmacodynamic studies were submitted to support the axSpA indication.

## 2.3.4. PK/PD modelling

The final population PK model was used to derive the individual exposure parameters required for the evaluation of the exposure-response relationships. There were 460 patients included in the efficacy E-R analyses (randomised to either filgotinib 200 mg q.d (N=349) or placebo (N=111)) for whom the PD endpoints were assessed at Week 16. In the safety E-R analyses, there were 475 patients included (treated with filgotinib 200 mg q.d (N=349) or placebo (N=126)) for whom the safety PD endpoints were assessed over 16 weeks. Effective AUC (AUCeff) was considered relevant for the exploration of efficacy endpoints, while Cmax,ss and AUC0τ,ss of filgotinib and AUCeff at steady-state were explored for safety endpoints. AUCeff can be considered a measure of overall pharmacologic activity following filgotinib

administration, and was derived from the following formula: where MW was the molecular weight of filgotinib and its metabolite and 0.1 reflects the lower potency of GS-829845 relative to filgotinib. Relationships were investigated through graphical explorations and logistic regression, with the relevant PK metric (AUCeff, AUC0-τ,ss, Cmax,ss) as the predictor. For the logistic regressions, maximum likelihood estimation was used. The influence of the covariates gender, weight, bDMARDNaïve and bDMARD-experienced, radiographic and non-radiographic were investigated on model parameters. Comparison of structural models was based on the Akaike Information Criteria (AIC) and diagnostic plots. Results efficacy Graphical exploration of exposure in filgotinib-treated patients who were ASAS40 responders or nonresponders showed no clear difference in AUCeff levels. Logistic regression modeling demonstrated that AUCeff is a significant predictor of likelihood of response. Further, the analysis found a significant interaction between sex and exposure (AUCeff) on ASAS40 response. In females, AUCeff distributions were similar between responders and non-responders, indicating no exposure-response signal. In males, higher AUCeff was observed in responders, indicating a potential exposure-response relationship in males. However, when exposure AUCeff medians are compared, the trend disappears, suggesting that this interaction may be driven by outliers. Although a statistically significant sex/exposure interaction was included in the model, predicted event probabilities and treatment odds ratios were similar between males and females across the observed exposure range (odds ratio for female and male subjects, versus placebo were 1.50 (95% CI 1.12; 1.99) and 1.44 (95% CI 1.11; 1.86), respectively). Therefore, this interaction did not appear to result in clinically meaningful differences in response between sexes. Results safety Over 16 weeks of treatment, only 4 SAE were reported (2 for active group and 2 for placebo group) therefore, no further logistic regression or E-R analysis were based on this parameter. A graphical exploration of AUCeff, Cmax,fil or AUCτ,fil at steady-state for participants experiencing ≥ 1 AUCeff = AUCfilgotinib + AUCGS-829845 * 0.1 * (MWfilgotib/MWGS-829845)

TEAE over 16 weeks; and those who did not; were generated using only the active participants. No clear difference in AUCs and Cmax levels were observed between the two groups. Results of the logistic regression model showed only the intercept was statistically significant but not the slope, suggesting that the probability of experiencing ≥ 1 TEAE within 16 weeks was not adequately predicted by the filgotinib AUCeff, Cmax,fil or AUCτ,fil at steady-state. As there was no evidence of association between TEAE experience and tested PK exposure metrics, no further exploration of covariates was conducted.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.3.5. Discussion on clinical pharmacology

The bioanalytical methods for filgotinib and its major metabolite were adequately validated, and the performance deemed acceptable. The main investigation of filgotinib's PK properties was assessed and reported in the initial MAA. The filgotinib 200 mg q.d. dose was the selected dose for the double-blind period (up to Week 16) in the Phase 3 study (GLPG0634-CL-336; OLINGUITO) study in subjects with axSpA, based on the favourable efficacy and safety results of the completed 12-week Phase 2 (GLPG0634-CL-223; TORTUGA) study in subjects with active AS. The objectives of the current population PK analysis were to characterise the population PK of filgotinib and its metabolite GS-829845, and to quantify the impact of significant covariates on the PK of filgotinib and GS-829845 in patients with axSpA. The analysis of the Phase 3 data in patients with axSpA was done using a population PK model previously developed on data from 16 clinical studies conducted in HV participants and RA, UC, CD, and axSpA patients (phase 2 data), with a reduced covariate model, as a starting point for the analysis. This approach was considered reasonable. As a full covariate analysis had been previously done, and the clinically relevant covariates identified, the covariate analysis focused on covariates relevant for patients with axSpA. The overall model development approach was considered reasonable. The additional covariate re-assessment did not reveal any additional significant covariates compared with the previously developed model, where the covariates dose, bodyweight (fixed exponents of 0.75 and 1) and eGFR were identified as relevant. The NCA analysis indicates only minor differences in exposure across indications, mainly for subjects with r-axSpA who appear to have a slightly lower exposure of filgotinib and the metabolite, however, that is based on a limited number of subjects and the variability is high. Disease (axSpA) was not identified as a significant covariate for either filgotinib or the metabolite. No correlation between parameter estimates and baseline CRP were identified. An under-prediction of high concentrations of filgotinib and GS-829845 was noted in the overall patient population, and of filgotinib for the axSpA population while there appeared to be a slight over-prediction of the lower concentrations. The variability was overpredicted for healthy participants and patients; however, the median appeared captured for the pooled patient population. The population parameters were derived with good precision (RSE&lt;10%) and the condition number was low indicating that the model is not over-parameterised or numerically instable. It is noted that negative shrinkage is estimated for several parameters. However, the negative values were close to zero, which indicates that was likely due to the estimation algorithms (SAEM followed by IMP) and/or study design, rather than a signal of model misspecification. The population model was used for descriptive purposes, and the exposure-response analysis was supportive only. The model was considered acceptable for deriving individual exposures for axSpA patients to be used in the exposure-response analysis. The information on exposure in patients described in the SmPC Section 5.2 is summarised based NCA analysis of the data from the PK sub-study (intensive sampling). This was acceptable. The population PK model was used to derive individual exposures for the exposure-response analysis. The relationships between exposure-efficacy and safety were investigated through graphical explorations and logistic regression, with the relevant PK metrics (AUCeff, AUC0-τ,ss, Cmax,ss) as the predictor. All patients included in the ER analysis had received a dose of 200 mg for 16 weeks, or placebo. The efficacy E-R modelling results showed a significant relationship between AUCeff and ASAS40 response at Week 16, including a significant sex and AUCeff interaction which was deemed to not result in clinically meaningful differences in response between sexes.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The exposure-safety analysis results did not show a significant relationship between AUCeff, Cmax,fil or AUCτ,fil and the likelihood of reporting ≥1 TEAE through Week 16. A limitation of these analyses was that only one dose was studied, resulting in narrow overall exposure range.

## 2.3.6. Conclusions on clinical pharmacology

The clinical pharmacology of filgotinib and its metabolite in the axSpA population has been adequately characterized.

## 2.4. Clinical efficacy

## 2.4.1. Dose response study(ies)

No formal dose response study was conducted in axSpA. The proof-of-concept phase 2 study is described in the section related to supplementary study.

## 2.4.2. Main study(ies)

GLPG0634-CL-336 (OLINGUITO): A Phase 3 randomised, placebo-controlled, double-blind, parallel-group programme to evaluate efficacy and safety of filgotinib in adult subjects with active axial spondyloarthritis

## Methods

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 6. Schematic study design

<!-- image -->

OLINGUITO is an ongoing Phase 3 programme consisting of 2 randomised, double-blind, placebocontrolled, multicenter, parallel-group studies (Study A and Study B) to evaluate the efficacy, safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of orally administered filgotinib for 16 weeks (double-blind treatment period) followed by an open-label treatment period, a dose de-escalation period (in subjects aged &lt;65 years and without pre-specified risk factors), and an OLE period (in selected countries).

The pre-specified risk factors were history of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (i.e. myocardial infarction, [un]stable angina pectoris, or coronary artery procedures, a previous venous thromboembolism), a prior malignancy or non-melanoma skin cancer, or other major risk factors for these events (such as current or past smoking [&gt;10 pack-years].

Based on the eligibility criteria for r-axSpA or nr-axSpA, subjects were allocated to either Study A or Study B. Eligible subjects in each study (A and B) were randomised in a 1:1 ratio to receive filgotinib 200 mg or placebo q.d. for 16 weeks during the double-blind treatment period. The randomization was stratified by high-sensitivity C-reactive protein (hs-CRP) ≤5 mg/L or &gt;5 mg/L at screening and prior use of biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drug(s) (bDMARD[s]) (bDMARD-naïve/non-inadequate responder [IR] versus bDMARD[s]-IR). Approximately 75% of the subjects were aimed to be bDMARDnaïve/non-IR and up to 25% of the subjects were aimed to be bDMARD(s)-IR and must have received not more than 2 bDMARDs.

Subjects who completed the double-blind treatment period and who did not permanently discontinue investigational product (IP) intake continued in the open-label treatment period in which all subjects aged &lt;65 years and without pre-specified risk factors received filgotinib 200 mg q.d. up to Week 52. Subjects aged ≥65 years and subjects with pre-specified risk factors who did not permanently discontinue IP intake are treated with their lowest effective dose (i.e. filgotinib 100 mg or 200 mg q.d.) up to Week 104, based on their response. Subjects aged &lt;65 years and without pre-specified risk factors who completed the open-label treatment period including the Week 52 required assessments, and who achieved sustained Ankylosing Spondylitis Disease Activity Score with C-reactive protein (ASDASCRP) &lt;2.1 at Week 40 and Week 52, were re-randomized (1:1) at Week 52 to receive either double-blind filgotinib 100 mg or 200 mg q.d. up to Week 104 in the dose de-escalation period. There is no dose de-escalation period for subjects aged ≥65 years and for subjects with pre-specified risk factors. The open-label treatment period is extended to Week 104 for this population. As from Week 104, subjects regardless of age and presence of pre-specified risk factors, who did not permanently discontinue IP intake, will be treated (i.e. filgotinib 100 mg or 200 mg q.d.) in the OLE based on their response1 up to Week 234 or until filgotinib becomes commercially available, whichever comes first, for both r-axSpA and nr-axSpA in their country. The dose de-escalation period and the OLE period are not in scope of this submission.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Study participants

## Selected inclusion criteria:

- Have an established diagnosis of axSpA by a rheumatologist (or other specialist with expertise in diagnosing axSpA).
- o Study A (r-axSpA): Meet ASAS classification criteria with radiographic sacroiliitis on X ray as follows:
- History of back pain &gt;=12 weeks and age at onset of back pain &lt;45 years, AND
- Have radiographic bilateral grade 2-4 sacroiliitis or unilateral grade 3-4 sacroiliitis, based on New York grading system, confirmed by central reading. Historical radiographs up to 6 months old were considered appropriate if they are accepted by the central reader. Otherwise, a new radiograph were obtained during the screening period, AND
- &gt;=1 spondyloarthritis (SpA) feature *.
- o Study B (nr-axSpA): Meet ASAS classification criteria without radiographic sacroiliitis on X ray as follows:
- History of back pain &gt;= 12 weeks and age at onset of back pain &lt;45 years, AND
- No radiographic bilateral grade 2-4 sacroiliitis or unilateral grade 3-4 sacroiliitis, AND
- Presence of sacroiliitis on MRI (based on central reading) and at least 1 SpA feature* or when positive for HLA B27: having at least 2 SpA features*, AND
- Have objective signs of inflammation, by sacroiliitis on MRI or elevated CRP

*SpA features: inflammatory back pain, arthritis, enthesitis, uveitis, dactylitis, psoriasis, Crohn's/colitis, good response to NSAIDs, family history for SpA, HLA B27, elevated CRP.

- Have active axSpA at screening and Day 1 defined by:
- o BASDAI &gt;=4 (numeric rating scale [NRS] 0 10), AND
- o Spinal pain score &gt;=4 (0 10 NRS) (based on BASDAI question 2).
- Have a history of inadequate response to &gt;=2 NSAIDs at the maximum dose of NSAIDs used in axSpA for &gt;=2 weeks each (a total duration of NSAID trial &gt;=4 weeks) or intolerance to &gt;=2 NSAIDs for the treatment of axSpA.
- Subjects designated as bDMARD(s) inadequate responder (IR) must have received not more than 2 bDMARD(s), that was/were administered in accordance with its/their labelling and discontinued due to:
- o Non-response (primary or secondary) after a minimum treatment of 12 weeks, and /or
- o Intolerance (defined as having experienced an adverse reaction [e.g. an infusion/injection reaction, an infection, a laboratory test change, etc] irrespective of treatment duration)
- If continuing csDMARD(s) during the study, subjects were permitted to use only a maximum of 2 of the following drugs and to have been on this treatment for &gt;=12 weeks prior to screening, with a stable dose and route of administration (defined as no change in prescription) for &gt;4 weeks prior to Day 1: methotrexate (MTX) oral or SC &lt;=25 mg/week, Leflunomide &lt;=20 mg/day, orally (may not be combined with MTX during the study), sulfasalazine &lt;=3 g/day, orally, hydroxychloroquine &lt;=400 mg/day or chloroquine &lt;=250 mg/day.
- Subjects who were already taking corticosteroids (not injectables) could participate in the study: they had to be on a stable dose of &lt;=7.5 mg/day of prednisone or equivalent for &gt;4 weeks prior to Day 1.
- If using allowed analgesics or non-drug therapies (including physiotherapies) for axSpA or nonprohibited concomitant medications for any reason, these were kept stable as defined the protocol.
- Ambulatory female or male subjects &gt;=18 years of age.
- Female subjects of childbearing potential had to have a negative highly sensitive pregnancy test during screening and to have agreed to continue monthly urine dipstick pregnancy testing during IP treatment and agreed to use contraception/preventive exposure measures.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Selected Exclusion Criteria

- Prior exposure to a JAKi, investigational or approved, at any time, including filgotinib.
- Use of any opioid analgesic at average daily doses &gt;30 mg/day of morphine (or equivalent) or use of unstable doses of any opioid analgesic &lt;=2 weeks prior to Day 1.
- Use of any of the following systemic immunomodulating therapies &lt;= 4 weeks prior to Day 1, including, but not limited to: 6 mercaptopurine, azathioprine, cyclosporine or other calcineurin inhibitors (e.g. sirolimus, tacrolimus), MTX if being discontinued, mycophenolate, antimalarials (e.g. hydroxychloroquine, chloroquine) if being discontinued, or SSZ if being discontinued.
- Administration of a live attenuated vaccine &lt;=4 weeks prior to Day 1, or planned during the study.
- Complete spinal ankylosis, defined as the presence of consecutive bridging syndesmophytes in &gt;=5 segments on the lateral radiograph (assessed by the central reader).
- Have undergone surgical treatments for peripheral manifestation of axSpA, including synovectomy or arthroplasty, or major surgery (requiring regional block or general anesthesia) &lt;=12 weeks prior to Day 1 or planned major surgery during the study.
- Have a diagnosis of any generalized musculoskeletal disorder, e.g. generalised osteoarthritis, or systemic inflammatory condition other than axSpA such as, but not limited to, gout, RA, juvenile chronic arthritis, psoriatic arthritis, reactive arthritis, Synovitis, Acne, Pustulosis, Hyperostosis, Osteitis (SAPHO) Syndrome, systemic lupus erythematosus, systemic vasculitis, scleroderma, inflammatory myopathy, mixed connective tissue disease, fibromyalgia, postacute coronavirus 19 (COVID 19) syndrome, and any overlapping syndrome.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Note: Prior history of reactive or other types of inflammatory arthritis was permitted if there was documentation of change in diagnosis to axSpA or additional diagnosis of axSpA.

- Have active CD or active UC.

Note: subjects may be enrolled if they had had a history of IBD, including CD and UC, but have had no exacerbation within 6 months prior to Day 1, and, if currently on treatment, had to be on stable treatment for &gt;=6 months prior to Day 1 and this treatment could be allowed per protocol.

- Active autoimmune disease other than those listed above, that would interfere with assessment of study parameters or increase risk to the subject by participating in the study (e.g. uncontrolled uveitis, uncontrolled thyroiditis, transverse myelitis, current peptic ulcer disease or prior history of severe diverticulitis [i.e. requiring hospitalization] or previous gastrointestinal perforation), per judgment of investigator.
- History of opportunistic infection, or immunodeficiency syndrome, which would put the subject at risk, as per investigator judgment.
- Active infection that is clinically significant, as per judgment of the investigator, or history of a serious infection (requiring hospitalization or systemic antibiotics) within 12 weeks prior to screening.
- Currently on any therapy for chronic infection (such as pneumocystis, cytomegalovirus, herpes simplex, herpes zoster and atypical mycobacteria).
- History of symptomatic herpes zoster or herpes simplex infection within 12 weeks prior to screening or have history of disseminated or complicated herpes zoster infection (e.g. multidermatomal, ophthalmic or central nervous system involvement) at any time.
- History of an infected joint prosthesis with the prosthesis still in situ.
- History of disseminated Staphylococcus aureus.
- Subject has evidence of active hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection: subjects with positive hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) at screening are excluded from the study. Subjects with positive hepatitis B core antibody (HbcAb) and negative HbsAg require reflex testing for HBV DNA. Subjects with positive HBV DNA at screening will be excluded. Subjects with positive HbcAb and negative HBV DNA are eligible per investigator judgment but may require prophylactic treatment in accordance with HBV treatment guidelines/local standard of care and require ongoing viral monitoring with blood tests for HBV DNA, as outlined in Appendix 4 of the protocol (Appendix 16.1.1).
- Subject has chronic hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection, as defined by positive HCV antibody (Ab) at screening and detectable HCV viremia. Subjects with positive HCV Ab must undergo reflex HCV RNA testing, and subjects with HCV RNA positivity will be excluded. Subjects with positive HCV Ab and negative HCV RNA are eligible per investigator judgment but require ongoing viral monitoring with blood tests for HCV RNA, as outlined in Appendix 4 of the protocol.
- Subject has a history of or a current immunosuppressive condition (e.g. human immunodeficiency virus [HIV] infection).
- History of organ or bone marrow transplant.
- Evidence of a history of or currently active tuberculosis (TB) (regardless of treatment):
- o positive QuantiFERON TB test result, orchest radiograph (posterior anterior view), taken within 12 weeks prior to or at screening and read by a qualified radiologist or pulmonologist, with evidence of current active TB infection, or
- o symptoms suggestive of clinically significant illness with TB in the 12 weeks before the initial IP administration.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Note: Subjects with a latent TB infection (LTBI) may be permitted into the study with sponsor approval if:

- o Subject previously treated for LTBI, i.e. if a subject has previously completed an adequate course of therapy for LTBI, no QuantiFERON ® TB test needs to be obtained, but a chest radiograph must be obtained (if not done within 12 weeks before screening, or if results are not available at the investigational site), without evidence of active or old inactive lesions.
- o Subject with newly identified LTBI, i.e. a subject who has a newly identified positive diagnostic TB test result (defined as any positive QuantiFERON ® TB test or an indeterminate test result on 2 separate QuantiFERON ® TB tests) for whom active TB has been ruled out, including having a negative chest radiograph, and for whom appropriate treatment for LTBI has been initiated, continued for at least 4 weeks prior to the first IP administration and if appropriate treatment is ongoing at screening and planned to be continued during the study until treatment is completed, in accordance with local/international guidelines.

Subject presenting any signs or symptoms of severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS Cov 2) infection, as detected at baseline following careful physical examination (e.g. cough, fever, headaches, fatigue, dyspnea, myalgia, anosmia, dysgeusia, anorexia, sore throat, etc.) (BMJ 2020), should undergo testing, even if fully vaccinated against SARS CoV 2, as per locally applicable standard criteria to diagnose SARS CoV 2 infection, and be excluded if positive.

- Subject had a history of malignancy or myelo or lymphoproliferative disorder, including nonmelanoma skin cancer (NMSC), excised and curatively treated non metastatic basal cell carcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma of the skin, or in situ uterine cervical carcinoma within the past 5 years prior to screening.
- Subject had a history or presence of clinically significant abnormalities detected on 12 lead electrocardiogram (ECG) of either rhythm or conduction e.g. known long QT syndrome or a QTcF &gt;450 ms detected on the 12 lead ECG. A first-degree atrioventricular block will not be considered as a significant abnormality.
- Subject had any other condition for which, in the opinion of the investigator, participation would not be in the best interest of the subject (e.g. compromise the well-being) or that could prevent, limit, or confound the protocol specified assessments.
- For subjects at increased risk of major cardiovascular problems (such as heart attack or stroke), those who smoke or have done so for a long time in the past (&gt;10 pack years) and those at increased risk of cancer, the investigator carefully considered if participation is in the best interest of the subject.
- The results of the following laboratory tests performed at the central laboratory at screening met any of the criteria below:
- d. Alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt;=1.5x upper limit of normal
- e. Total bilirubin level &gt;=2x ULN unless the subject has been diagnosed with Gilbert's disease and this

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

a. Haemoglobin &lt;8.0 g/dL (international system of unit [SI]: &lt;80 g/L); b. Neutrophils &lt;1.0 x 10 3 cells/mm 3 (SI: &lt;1.0 x 10 9 cells/L); c. Lymphocytes &lt;0.5 x 10 3 cells/mm 3 (SI: &lt;0.5 x 10 9 cells/L); (ULN); is clearly documented;

- f. Creatinine clearance &lt;60 mL/min by Cockcroft Gault equation.
- Subject has a known hypersensitivity to IP ingredients or history of a significant allergic reaction to IP ingredients as determined by the investigator or a known hereditary problem of galactose intolerance, total lactase deficiency, or glucose galactose malabsorption.
- Contraindication to MRI or any condition that would interfere with the ability to perform an MRI.
- Female subject was pregnant or breast feeding or intending to become pregnant or breastfeed during the study or within 35 days after last dose of IP.
- Investigator or other study staff or relative thereof who was directly involved in the conduct of the study.
- Subject had any condition or circumstances that, in the opinion of the investigator, may make a subject unlikely or unable to complete the study or comply with study procedures and requirements (e.g. active alcohol or drug abuse).
- Subject was institutionalized by virtue of an order issued by either the judicial or the administrative authorities or had a dependence on the sponsor or investigator.

## Treatments

The study medication comprised 100 mg and 200 mg filgotinib tablets for oral administration, and placebo to match (PTM) 100 mg and 200 mg filgotinib tablets for oral administration. Double-blind Treatment Period (up to Week 16) Doses of 200 mg filgotinib or PTM were taken q.d. orally up to Week 16. Subjects were provided with blinded medication kits containing either 200 mg filgotinib tablets or PTM 200 mg filgotinib tablets. At the baseline visit (Day 1), IP was administered on site after predose assessments had been completed. Starting from Day 2, subjects were instructed to take 1 tablet q.d. Open-label Treatment Period (After Week 16 and up to Week 52) For continued q.d. treatment after Week 16 up to Week 52 or Week 104 (depending on age and the presence of pre-specified risk factors), subjects are provided with open label medication kits containing 100 mg or 200 mg filgotinib tablets. The following instructions for the use of rescue medication for the treatment were provided in the protocol:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- ≤Week 16:

NSAIDs, (opioid) analgesics, corticosteroids, or csDMARD therapy: not to be adjusted. If the initiation or dose adjustments of NSAID, (opioid) analgesics, corticosteroids, or csDMARD therapy was required for axSpA symptoms prior to Week 16, the subject continued IP at the discretion of the investigator and will be encouraged to continue the study as per protocol. bDMARDs or JAKi's were not to be started. If the initiation of a bDMARD or a JAKi was required for axSpA symptoms, prior to Week 16, the subject discontinued IP but was encouraged to continue with study visits and assessments. At Week 52 the latest, the subject discontinued the study.

- &gt;Week 16:

Adjustments permitted: Modifying dose of existing medication(s) and/or introducing new medication(s) (NSAID, [opioid] analgesics and/or csDMARDs, respecting the maximal doses as described in the protocol). Subjects should not have been dosed with rescue therapy for axSpA and/or occasional use for other reasons with NSAID and/or (opioid) analgesics, within 12 hours prior to a study visit. Intra-articular corticosteroids (injected) for treatment for peripheral manifestations: dose should not have exceeded the equivalent dose of triamcinolone 80 mg suspension. For the analysis of the SJC44, TJC44 and TDC, these joints were considered not assessable\" for 12 weeks from the time of the intraarticular injection and had to be documented. Not permitted:

- If there was a need for the use of oral or parenteral corticosteroids (dose &gt;7.5 mg/day) for axSpA after Week 16, the subject discontinued IP but was encouraged to continue with study visits and assessments. At Week 52, the subject discontinued the study.
- If bDMARD or JAKi therapy was required after Week 16, the subject discontinued IP but was encouraged to continue with study visits and assessments. At Week 52, the subject discontinued the study.

Although the use of rescue medications was permitted, taking the above-mentioned restrictions into account, the use of certain rescue medications was scheduled, if possible, for at least 12 hours prior to a study visit. In case of loss of ASAS40 response for subjects aged 65 years or above and for subjects with prespecified risk factors, protocol-allowed rescue medication was optimised, if deemed useful by the investigator, before the dose is increased to 200 mg filgotinib q.d. In case a filgotinib dose increase to 200 mg q.d. was considered needed and the subject had already taken his/her daily dose from a 100 mg open-label medication kit on the day of confirmed loss of ASAS40 response, the intake of openlabel filgotinib 200 mg started the next day. Should, after increase to filgotinib 200 mg q.d., a subject achieves a stable ASAS40 response per investigator's judgment, dose reduction was considered. If the subject had already taken filgotinib 200 mg, the intake of open-label filgotinib 100 mg started the next day.

## Definition of the primary and multiplicity controlled secondary endpoints:

ASAS20 and ASAS40 assess 4 domains: the \"Patient Global Assessment of Disease\", Spinal Pain (first

question from the spinal pain assessment), Physical Function (based on BASFI) and Morning Stiffness (based on mean of BASDAI questions 5 and 6). The ASDASCRP endpoint was calculated and derived from several PROs and hs-CRP. For this calculation, the investigator was responsible to ensure completeness of the PROs and appropriately conduct the assessments that comprise this endpoint.

The 5 domains included in ASDASCRP are:

- Total back pain on 0-10 scale (based on BASDAI question 2)
- Patient's global assessment of disease activity on 0-10 scale
- Peripheral joint pain and/or swelling on 0-10 scale (based on BASDAI question 3)
- Duration of morning stiffness on 0-10 scale (based on BASDAI question 6)
- hs-CRP in mg/L.

The ASDAS has a continuous scale from 0 with no defined upper end. Following is the formula used for calculating the ASDASCRP (Machado et al. 2010): ASDASCRP = 0.121 × Back Pain + 0.058 × Duration of Morning Stiffness + 0.110 × Patient Global + 0.073 × Peripheral Pain/Swelling + 0.579×Ln(hs-CRP mg/L+1) (Sieper et al. 2009).

ASDASCRP change from baseline, low disease activity (LDA), ID, major improvement and clinically important improvement will be calculated from the ASDASCRP data. The BASDAI is a validated questionnaire that consists of 6 questions pertaining to the 5 major symptoms of AS: fatigue, spinal pain, peripheral arthritis, enthesitis, intensity of morning stiffness and

duration of morning stiffness. Each question will be rated using a numerical rating scale from 0 (none)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

to 10 (very severe). The BASDAI score is calculated by computing the mean of questions 5 and 6 and adding it to the sum of questions (Q)1-4. This score is then divided by 5. The BASFI is a set of 10 questions designed to determine the degree of functional limitation in those with AS. The 10 questions were chosen with a major input from patients with AS. The first 8 questions consider activities related to functional anatomy. The final 2 questions assess the subjects' ability to cope with everyday life. A 0-10 numerical rating scale is used to answer the questions with 0 being \"Easy\" and 10 being \"Impossible.\" The score will be derived from the mean of the 10 items by the sponsor and is indicative of the subject's level of ability. The ASQoL assesses health-related quality of life in AS patients. The questionnaire consists of 18 yes/no questions. A single point will be assigned for each \"yes\" response, resulting in a total score between 0 and 18, with lower scores indicating a better quality of life. The BASMI combines 5 measures: lateral spinal flexion, tragus-to-wall distance, lumbar flexion (modified Schober index), maximal intermalleolar distance, and cervical rotation: MRI images of the spine and SIJs will be scored by a central reader using the SPARCC scoring system. The scores as provided by the central reader will be used in the analysis, which will focus on the changes from baseline.

## Sample size

When assuming that the proportion of subjects achieving ASAS40 at Week 16 would be 45% and 25% for the filgotinib and placebo groups, respectively, 119 subjects per arm would be required to achieve 90% power at a 2-sided 5% significance level in each of Study A and Study B. The sponsor was to monitor the treatment discontinuation rate during the study, including those discontinuations due to SARS-CoV-2. If this rate were higher than anticipated (for reasons other than safety), the sponsor could randomise additional subjects. This decision was to be taken in a blinded manner following the recommendations given in Section 4.4 of the ICH E9 guideline. Regulatory authorities and ethics committees were to be informed as applicable and per local regulations.

## Randomisation

At screening, subjects were assigned a subject identification number. Based on the eligibility criteria for r-axSpA or nr-axSpA, subjects were assigned to either Study A or Study B. Double-blind treatment period and open-label treatment period At baseline, in each Study A and B, subjects were randomised in a 1:1 ratio to filgotinib 200 mg or placebo q.d. for a total of 16 weeks. Allocation of each subject to a given treatment were done using a centralised electronic system. The randomisation was stratified by hs-CRP ≤5 mg/L or &gt;5 mg/L at screening and prior use of bDMARD(s) (bDMARD-naïve/non-IR versus bDMARD(s)-IR) to ensure treatment balance in each stratum. Subjects who completed IP intake in the 16-week double-blind period continued in the open label treatment period in which all subjects received filgotinib 100 mg or 200 mg q.d. up to Week 52 or Week 104, depending on age and the presence of pre-specified risk factors. Dose de-escalation period Subjects aged &lt;65 years and without pre-specified risk factors from Study A and Study B who achieved sustained ASDASCRP &lt;2.1 at Weeks 40 and 52 were re-randomised at Week 52 to doubleblind filgotinib 100 mg or 200 mg q.d. and entered the dose de-escalation period up to Week 104. Allocation of each subject to a given treatment were done using a centralised electronic system

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(RTSM). Stratification, per Study A and B, were based on the disease activity measured at Week 52, as follows: ASDASCRP ID (ASDASCRP &lt;1.3) versus ASDASCRP LDA (ASDASCRP ≥1.3 and &lt;2.1) to ensure treatment balance in each stratum.

## Blinding (masking)

The subjects and study personnel (including the investigators and clinical study coordinators) were blinded to the initial treatment assignment until the Week 52 analysis. They remained blinded to the re-randomisation assignment in the dose de-escalation period through the entire duration of the study, with the exception of the sponsor's clinical study supply leader. The sponsor study team was unblinded to the initial treatment assignment in the double-blind treatment period at the time of the primary analysis, i.e. once all randomised subjects in the overall programme (both Study A and Study B) had completed their Week 52 visit or discontinued from the study earlier. At the end of the 16-week double-blind treatment period, all subjects who completed IP intake were assigned to open-label filgotinib 100 mg or 200 mg q.d. up to Week 52 or Week 104, depending on age and the presence of pre-specified risk factors. All IP configurations in the double-blind treatment period and open-label treatment period were identical in appearance, shape, and packaging. The blind could be broken only if the investigator deemed it necessary for the safety of a subject. The investigator was encouraged to discuss considerations to break the blind with the medical monitor of the study, whenever possible and where the situation allowed. The blind could be broken by the

investigator via a centralized electronic system (randomization and trial supply management [RTSM]).

## Statistical methods

This assessment report is based on the results of the Week 16 primary analysis and the final Week 52 analysis, performed once all subjects had completed their Week 52 assessments or discontinued earlier. The primary analysis and all other analyses were performed and presented separately for each Study A and Study B. For efficacy parameters and subject information (except for exposure), the treatment group as assigned by the randomisation was used in the analysis (i.e. as randomised analysis), using the stratification reported in RTSM. Primary analyses for the primary and secondary endpoints were repeated using stratum derived from CRF data. An additional analysis was performed using stratum derived from CRF data and the actual r-axSpA/nr-axSpA classification. Analysis sets

The following analysis sets were defined for efficacy evaluation:

- The All Randomised Analysis Set included all screened subjects who were randomized into the clinical study.
- The Full Analysis Set included all randomised subjects who were administered IP at least once.
- The Open-Label Full Analysis Set included all subjects randomised in the double-blind treatment period who were administered open-label IP at least once.

Unless otherwise specified, all efficacy analyses were carried out using the Full Analysis Set population. Efficacy analyses related to the open-label treatment period only were carried out using the OpenLabel Full Analysis Set population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Analysis methods

Primary endpoint ASAS40 response at Week 16: The comparison between filgotinib and placebo was presented as the number (percent) of responders with a 95% Clopper-Pearson CI per treatment arm alongside risk differences with a 95% CI using normal approximation. The difference between treatment groups at Week 16 was tested via a Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) test stratified by Screening hs-CRP and Prior bDMARD(s) factors within each of Study A and Study B. Secondary Efficacy Parameters: The difference between treatment arms in mean change from baseline at Week 16 was compared via an MMRM that included fixed effects for treatment, time point, treatment-by-time point, baseline value, and randomisation strata for Screening hs-CRP and bDMARDs. This model used an unstructured variance-covariance matrix and the Kenward Rogers approximation for denominator degrees of freedom. Fallback strategies in case of non-convergence were specified in the SAP. When only one post-baseline time point was planned (e.g. for SPARCC MRI Score of SIJs) an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model with similar fixed effects but excluding time covariates and repeated measures was used instead. LS means and 95% CIs, as well as LS mean differences with associated 95% CI and p-values between treatment groups were presented for each

parameter.

## Estimands and Handling of intercurrent events

The primary main estimand was described according to the following attributes:

- Treatment: Filgotinib 200 mg or placebo
- Population: Subjects with r-axSpA or nr-axSpA, for each study respectively, as defined by the inclusion and exclusion criteria.
- Variable: ASAS40 status (Yes/No) at Week 16
- Intercurrent events including strategy for addressing them:
- All available data values collected after IP discontinuation and/or use of rescue medication were disregarded and subjects considered nonresponders (composite)

Two supplementary estimands for the primary endpoint were also defined. They were identical to the primary estimand except for the handling of intercurrent events.

## Primary supplementary estimand 1:

- All available data values were used regardless of occurrence of any intercurrent events, such as premature IP discontinuation or use of rescue medication (treatment policy).

## Primary supplementary estimand 2:

- All data collected after treatment discontinuation were disregarded and subjects considered non-responders (composite).
- All data collected after any rescue medication up to Week 16 that did not lead to IP discontinuation were used in the analysis (treatment policy).

The secondary main estimand was described according to the following attributes:

- Treatment: Filgotinib 200 mg or placebo.
- Population: Subjects with r-axSpA or nr-axSpA, for each study respectively, as defined by the inclusion and exclusion criteria.
- Variable: ASDASCRP, SPARCC MRI score of SIJs, BASFI, ASQoL, or BASMI (linear) change from baseline at Week 16.
- Intercurrent events including strategy for addressing them:
- All available data values were used regardless of occurrence of any intercurrent events, such as premature IP discontinuation or use of rescue medication (treatment policy).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

One supplementary estimand for the secondary endpoints were also defined. It was identical to the primary estimand except for the handling of intercurrent events. Secondary supplementary estimand:

- All data collected after use of rescue medication were used in the statistical analysis
- All data that were not considered on-treatment (after IP discontinuation) were disregarded in the analysis and the last on-treatment value or baseline were used in the analysis.

Missing data and sensitivity analyses For binary endpoints (e.g. ASAS40), the main analysis used a Non-Responder Imputation (NRI) approach regardless of the strategy defined for intercurrent events. In this analysis, the absence of observed response is interpreted as non-response. A sensitivity analysis using multiple imputation (MI) under the MAR assumption was performed for the primary main estimand, where NRI was applied when subjects discontinued IP and/or used any rescue medication. This analysis evaluated whether study conclusions remained consistent when missing onIP data were imputed under the assumption that missing values follow similar trends within each treatment group (i.e. among subjects with similar baseline characteristics and observed outcomes). For the primary supplementary estimand 1 (treatment policy strategy, including all data regardless of IP discontinuation or use of any rescue medication), two sensitivity analyses were conducted: 1. MI under MAR, assuming missing data follow the same trend as observed data within each treatment group. 2. MI under MNAR using a copy increment from placebo approach, applying a less favorable assumption for filgotinib by imputing missing off IP data as if they followed the trajectory of subject treated with placebo after IP discontinuation. For the continuous secondary endpoints assessed at multiple postbaseline visits (i.e. ASDASCRP, BASFI, BASMI [linear], and ASQoL), the main analysis of the secondary main estimand used a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) under the MAR assumption, which utilizes all available data without imputation and provides unbiased estimates if data are missing at random. A sensitivity analysis under MNAR (copy increment from placebo) was conducted to assess robustness to departures from the MAR assumption. For the secondary endpoint of SPARCC MRI score of SIJs, which was assessed at a single postbaseline timepoint, the main analysis of the secondary main estimand used an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model. Missing data were handled using MI under the MAR assumption, and a corresponding sensitivity analysis under MNAR (copy increment from placebo) was performed to evaluate the robustness of the results. The model for MI imputation was further specified in the SAP. Additionally, a tipping point analysis was performed for the main estimands of the primary and secondary endpoints to evaluate whether the overall study conclusions would change under increasingly unfavourable assumptions for missing data in the filgotinib 200 mg group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Open-Label Period (up to Week 52) analysis For the open-label period, the main estimand handled ICEs in the following way:

- all data collected after any use of rescue medication were used in the statistical analysis.
- all data collected after IP discontinuation were disregarded and subjects were considered as non-responders.

A supplementary estimand was also defined which handled ICEs in the following way:

- all data collected after any use of rescue medication were disregarded and subjects were considered as non-responders.
- all data collected after IP discontinuation were disregarded and subjects were considered as non-responders.

For both estimands, missing observations for ASAS40 response were imputed as non-response (NRI). No week 52 endpoints were included in the multiple testing procedure. Multiplicity All hypothesis tests were performed at the primary analysis timepoint (Week 16) of the double-blind treatment period. Within each of Study A and Study B, the primary endpoint was tested at the 5% two-sided significance level. Secondary endpoints were tested only if statistical significance was shown for the primary endpoint in that study. Each subsequent endpoint (ASDASCRP change from baseline, SPARCC MRI of the SIJs score change from baseline) was only tested if the previous was significant at the 5% alpha level. If statistical significance was shown for SPARCC MRI of the SIJs, a step-down Bonferroni-Holm procedure was applied to control the family-wise error rate across the remaining secondary endpoints (ASQoL, BASFI, and BASMI [linear] definition 1 score [main] changes from baseline). Using this approach, the smallest p-value was tested at 1.67% (0.05/3). If significant, the next was tested at 2.5% (0.05/2). If also significant, the final one was tested at 5% (0.05/1). The testing sequence stopped at the first nonsignificant result. The complete multiplicity correction for primary and secondary endpoints within each study is summarized in the Figure below.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Interim analyses

No formal interim analysis was conducted in this study.

The study report was based on the results of the Week 16 primary analysis and the final Week 52 analysis of the study, performed once all randomized subjects in the overall programme (Study A and Study B) had completed their Week 52 visit or discontinued from the study earlier. The OLINGUITO study was ongoing to Week 104.

Per protocol, the clinical programme originally planned 2 \"interim analyses\" (the Week 16 primary analysis and the Week 52 analysis) and 1 final analysis.

Due to recruitment considerations and the timing of the full Week 52 data availability, the sponsor study team decided, before unblinding, to perform the primary analysis and the Week 52 analysis at the same time.

The final analysis will be performed once all subjects in the overall programme (Study A and Study B) have completed the study (i.e. last subject last visit in both studies, after Week 104).

## Recruitment

The study was conducted between 5 April 2023 (first subject first visit) and 22 May 2025 (Week 52 last subject last visit). In the overall programme (Study A and Study B combined), 19 countries screened subjects and subjects were randomised in 91 sites in 18 countries: Belgium, Bulgaria, the Czech Republic, Estonia, France, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Republic of Korea, Lithuania, the Netherlands, Philippines, Poland, Romania, South Africa, Spain, United Kingdom, and Taiwan. In addition, 1 site in Croatia had 1 screening failure only and did not enrol anyone.

Of the 915 subjects screened in the overall programme, 258 subjects were randomised in Study A and 237 subjects were randomised in Study B.

## Conduct of the study

## Changes in the Conduct of the Study

The protocol version 1.0 dated 20 July 2022 was the original protocol, which was amended 3 times during the clinical study. The key changes below were made to the original protocol:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Global Protocol Amendments

## Amendment 1 (protocol version 2.0), dated 18 April 2023

Following the outcome of the Article 20 procedure on JAK inhibitors for chronic inflammatory conditions (EMEA/H-A20/1517/C/005113/0014), a revised dose regimen for elderly and subjects with prespecified risk factors was introduced to mitigate the risks observed in the Oral Surveillance study considered JAKis class effects:

- All subjects ≥65 years and/or with pre-specified risk factors were treated with the lowest effective dose (100 or 200 mg q.d.) after Week 16, if they had been enrolled based on protocol version 2.0 or later. These subjects are marked with \"RTSM\" in the analyses.
- Addition of language to ascertain participation in the best interest of the subject for elderly and subjects with pre-specified risk factors.
- Eligibility criteria regarding exclusion of malignancy cases were made more stringent.
- Addition of Week 52 MRI assessment timepoint and of CANDEN and SPARCC SSS and assessment of the full study population (instead of a subpopulation of bDMARD naïve r-axSpA only).
- A number of changes were made in response to requests from health authorities, including, but not limited to:
- Updates to comply with ASAS EULAR 2022 recommendations for the management of axSpA (maximum dose of NSAID in inclusion criteria and addition of ASDAS at baseline)
- Addition of an IDMC in the study
- Revision of exclusion criteria (specification of a serious infection and galactose/lactose intolerance)

## Amendment 2 (protocol version 3.0), dated 11 August 2023

- Implementation of clarifications and input following feedback from investigators, including the revision of the study period to continue study visits and assessments up to Week 52 in case of IP discontinuation.
- Some eligibility criteria were clarified.
- The primary estimand was updated to consider a composite strategy to account predominantly for impact of the various effects in different rescue medications.

## Amendment 3 (protocol version 4.0), dated 27 March 2024

- Sponsor details were updated in scope of the sponsorship transfer of the study from Galapagos NV to Alfasigma S.p.A.
- A number of clarifications/corrections were made regarding the assessment of differential lymphocyte count and vital signs, blinding procedures, and interim analyses. In addition, UK specific protocol text was removed from the global protocol because a UK specific protocol version was in effect.

According to the applicant, the transfer of sponsorship of the study is not considered to have impacted the data integrity or the analyses. Clinical oversight on the study continued to be outsourced to the same vendor as well as the central laboratory remained the same. Medical monitoring activities remained with the same clinical vendor and the data management vendor remained the same as well. The study transfer has been conducted according to a mutually agreed and documented process, with focus on the continuation of study conduct without disruption and maintained data collection and management procedures.Changes in the Planned Analyses The SAP (version 1.0) dated 6 March 2024 was the original SAP, which was updated once during the clinical study (version 2.0, dated 2 July 2025). The following changes were made to the analyses described in the SAP but not covered by protocol amendments (prior to database lock and unblinding):

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Several analysis sets were (re)defined from the protocol version 3.0:
- Open Label Full Analysis Set was added for analyses of the open label treatment period.
- Pharmacodynamic and Pharmacokinetic/Pharmacodynamic Analysis Sets were removed as these data will be reported outside the CSR.
- Pharmacokinetic Analysis Set was updated by selecting all subjects with a plasma concentration available
- An additional Pharmacokinetic Analysis Set was created for the analysis of the PK substudy only.
- Per protocol, a long-term safety analysis over the 52-week double blind and open label treatment period was planned, but at the time of the primary analysis/Week 52 analysis, this analysis was part of the ISS combining all safety data from subjects exposed to filgotinib from Olinguito Study A/Study B and Tortuga studies.

Per the SAP, for standard non compartmental analysis of the PK sub-study data, AUCτ was to be calculated using imputed pre-dose data at 24 hours. However, for 3 subjects with pre-dose concentrations that were higher than their 6-hour post-dose concentrations, AUCτ was calculated by extrapolation as this was considered a more appropriate analysis. Cut-off date: 22MAY2025

Open-label Treatment Period (After Week 16 and up to Week 52)

A total of 95/251 subjects (37.8%) had at least 1 major protocol deviation during the open label treatment period. The most common major protocol deviation classifications (i.e. reported in ≥15 subjects overall) were visits and assessments (48 subjects, 19.1%), (non /) investigational medicinal product (43 subjects, 17.1%), and informed consent form (ICF; 23 subjects, 9.2%).

## Discrepancies in Study Allocation, Stratification, and Risk Factor Status

During data verification, before database lock, discrepancies were identified in Study A and Study B allocation and between the stratification factors and risk factor status recorded in the CRFs and those used in the randomization dataset (RTSM).

The randomisation was conducted using the stratification factors captured in RTSM. Based on review and sensitivity analyses performed for the primary and secondary efficacy endpoints, the discrepancies in study allocation and stratification were confirmed not to have a material impact on study population, randomization balance, or on the interpretation of efficacy outcomes.

Data reconciliation process and the number of affected subjects were documented in a Blinded Data Review Meeting Summary and are summarised below:

## Study Allocation

In total, 5 (1.9%) subjects were allocated to Study A (r-axSpA), who should have been assigned to Study B (nr-axSpA): 3 subjects in the filgotinib 200 mg group and 2 in the placebo group.

## Discrepancies in Stratification

- CRP strata: 2 subjects in the filgotinib 200 mg group had discrepancies between RTSM (used for stratification) and CRF; 1 was stratified to the ≤5 mg/L group but had &gt;5 mg/L per CRF, and 1 was stratified to the &gt;5 mg/L group but had ≤5 mg/L per CRF.
- bDMARD strata: 10 subjects were mis stratified. Of these, 6 subjects were stratified as IR (per RTSM) but should have been stratified as naïve/non IR (2 in the filgotinib 200 mg group and 4 in the placebo group), and 4 subjects were stratified as naïve/non IR but should have been stratified as IR (2 in each treatment group).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Discrepancies in Risk Factor Status

Per CRF, 72/258 (27.9%) subjects were aged ≥65 years and/or had pre-specified risk factors. Of these 72 subjects, 56 were identified as such at the start of the study (per RTSM) and were intended to be exposed to the lowest effective dose during the open label treatment period. Of the remaining 16 subjects, 4 had risk factors per the data collected but were incorrectly enrolled as non-risk subjects, and 12 were enrolled under the initial protocol, which did not differentiate by risk factor status.
